6,500-Year-Old 
Tomb Opened 


’ Amazing: Discovery 
Made In Egypt © 


Cairo, April. 15. 
An important archaeological dis- 


covery has been made in the Great}, 


Necropolis at Sakkara. 
-A burin) chamber has: been found, 
beautifully ' painted . and decorated 
with reliefs of fishes, birds and hunt- 
ing seenes, containing a sarcophagus 
and: the .body of Demed;.a nephew 
of TI)-a promincnt landowner of 
the fifth dynasty, about 4500 B.C, 

The discovery Is described as being 
of the greatest importance. It 
follows on unsuccessful search, over 
a period of twenty years, The, dis- 
coverer, Sctim Hassan, found a six~ 
teen yard shaft concealed behind a 
filse door to the Ti. Tomb, leading 
to Demed’s Tomb. . 

The chamber had been robbed In’ 
ancient times, and Demed's body was 
found lylng .on the floor.—Reuter

A. TRAGEDY OF 
HOLLYWOOD 
FORMER FILM-STAR’S 
10s. THEFT _ 


New_York, Apr. 10 

One of Hollywood's many trage-, 
dies—obscured by the huge salaries, 
bright Hebts, and glamour of the film 
capital—ls revealed by the trial of 
the Marchesa ‘Theodora Marcone for 
theft. 

It was not really a trial since the 
Marchesa, once the toast of Broad~- 
way ond one of the first women to 
make a name for herself in aviation, 
pleaded guilty to stcaling 2% dollars’ 
(10s.) worth of merchandise from 4 
Los Angeles shop. She threw her- 
self on the mercy of the Court, ask- 
ing for probation. 

. Judge Leroy Dawson heard her 
sympathetically, but discovered that 
she had no meuns of support, und £0 
ruled that she must. go to the county. 
poor farm, unless 15 doltars (£3) 
weekly can be provided to place her 
in a rehabilitation home. She Is be- 
ing kept in gaol while attempts are 
being made to interest welfare wor- 
kers in her case. : : 

Before her marriage to an Italian 
nobleman Theo Carew, as she was 
then known, ‘hod been a leading 
lady for John Drew: and won fame in 
tht carly days of films. She had 


-{been a friend of David Belasco, 


Henry Walthall, Mae Marsh, and the 
Glsh sisters. < . 
» After.a happy . marriage to an 

,{ Italian nobleman she settled in Italy, 
where on’ the ‘outbreak of the war 
her husband entered the Flying Ser- 
viee and she the’ Ambulance Corps. 
After his death she took to the. air, 
becoming, Italy's first .woman war 
flyer, After the war she participat- 
ed in one of the first olr races for 
women in Paris. She js now 51,

PROPOSED MENTAL 
HOSPITAL; 


KOWLOON SITE FOR BUILDING 
NOT ABANDONED 


Previous reports giving definiteness 
to an alleged Government decision 
not to uso the orca already prepared 
next to the Kowloon Hospital for 
the proposed new Mental Hospital 
which is to take the place of the 
present obsolete building at Salying- 
pun, have — been. discounted by in- 
quiries at the Public Works Depart- 
ment. '. ; 

On' the contrary, it is stated that 
the Kowloon site, which is quite a 
suitable one, ts still included in the 
tentatlve plans. These, {t would ap- 
pear, await the necessary funds be- 
fore the scheme enters into official 
estimates, . : 

Medical circles are optimistic of 
these funds becoming available very 
much sooner than expected, one doc- 
tor appearing to be well-informed 
when he, told a_ Press * repre- 
sentative yesterday that the Kowloon 
site has every chance of being chosen 
and the plan appearing Jn thts year's 
estimates, 


Present Homo a Disgraco 

The present Mental Hospital, which 
Is situated In'n nolsy slum locality: 
on the hill just above the Govern- 
ment’ Clvil- Hospital, dates back to 
some time after the completion of 
the latter institution—a . period of 
nbout 40 yenrs ago. The most It can’ 
accommodate is 50 inmates, and this 
has slnce been the number regularly 
cared for, the accommodation being 
So much taxed that In a month, be-- 
tween 10 and 20 other patients’ who 
cannot be admitted, have to be sent, 
to Canton, where they go Into the 
former John Kerr Hospital, an insti- 
tution Which has since been taken 
over by the Municipal Government. 
These transfers are only of Chinese, 
who are not of Hongkong or British 
citizenship,--—— —-— ---—-- 

On Wednesday at a meeting of the 
Legislatlve Council, His Excellency 
the Governor, Sir Andrew Caldecott, 
said, Jn referring to medical mat- 
ters, “The word “disgrace” {s, I re- 
fret to say, applicable also to ‘the 
premises which are at present dipni- 
fied by the name of “Mental Hos- 
pital.” The opening of the Queen 
‘Mary ‘and the closing of the old Civil 
Tospital - wil, leave these premises 
marooned, ond ‘f. commend to the 
attention of all concerned the neces- 
sity for thelr early replacement and 
for a change of site.” 


ZEISS IKON CAMERAS 


IMPROVEMENTS MADE DURING 
PAST TWELVE MONTHS 


Every year noveltics in the camera 
industry uppear on the market and 
proved types of cameras continually 
show further advances, ° 

During 1930,Zclss Ilcon has brought 
out many novelties and in the sphere 
of mininture cameras construction

THE FERRY MISHAP 


It requires no great stretch of ]. 


the imagination to pereeive that 
the collision between two “Star” 
Ferry Company vessels in mid- 
harbour on Wednesday evening 
might easily have been a major 
‘/catastrophe, resulting in heavy 
loss of life. As it was, thanks 
largely to the presence of mind 
of the coxswain of the “Night 
Star,” : not only were the 
nundreds of passengers ‘aboard 
the ferry-boat safely landed, but 
no injury was suffered by any- 
one. The vompany has a record, 
in the matter of freedom from 
mishaps, which is a credit to all 
concerned in the maintenance of 
a service which carries some 


rary HIS ‘is the story of 5 
~ EP. pint of milk, the pint:

I shadowed ,

and: night;

breakfast tables. 

:Milk {s.. the most perishable 
and most necessary foodstuff to 
human ‘beings — particularly 
small ones. Into’ London alone 
four million pints are imported 


from the surrounding country- 


side cach night and delivered to 
three. million homes each morn- 
ing. And milk is irreplaceable. 
No artificial substitute has been 
or is likely:to be discovered. It 
is one of Nature’s patents.

shire, halfway from Salisbury 
to the sea. The cow that ate

Shorthorn, one of cighty that

The cow was a mixed brown-

. The farmer here is one of the

broken even in his accounts.

might have been worse, and has 
no wish to change his job.

taking.

for attainments ‘in the ficld of art 
or science. The cash awards ore 
accompanied by gold medals of

publi¢ occasions.” ‘

turn ‘upon ‘consideration of Mona- 
wasque, French dnd English law.

é Sandwich, Mar. 25. 
Cambridge golfers avenged 
the Boat-race at Prince’s here 
this afternoon. They beat Ox- 
ford as expected in the singles 
by six games to three with one 
halved and, the foursomes yes- 
terday having been divided,

seven to four with four 
halved. .

JOCKEY’S CONDITION 


Hfs many friends will be pleased 
to learn that Mr. S. ¥. Liang, the 
popular local jockey, who met with 
a serious accident at,the race mect~ 
ing at Happy Valley last Saturday, 
hos shown a slight {mprovement. 
The danger however, is not yct over, 
actording to his medical advisers. 


Mr. Liang is being treated at the 
Hongkong Sahitorium and Hospital, 
where he was admitted immediately 
after the accident.

Lynching Bill 
Opposed 


Likely To Be Shelved 
By Senate 


Washington, April, 15. 


The House of Representatives to- 
day passed a bill, by 276 fo 119, ‘n= 
troduced by Mr. Joseph Gavayan, 
making lynching a federal offence. 


The bill ‘imposes heavy fines and 
imprisonment upon sheriffs or con< 
slables permitting their prisoners to 
be taken from custody and killed, 
while individuals composing the 
lynching mobs would be similarly 
punished. 


The medusure ‘was bitterly opposed 
by Democrat representatives fron: 
ihe southern states“and It Is believed 
likely It will be shelved in the Senate. 
—Reuer. =

Little U.S. © 
Champ Beats 
Harry Mizler 


Harringay Stadium, Apr. 15. 
The American featherweight cham~- 


-|pion, Petey Sarron, in a ten-round 


Nght here to-night, defented Harry 
Mizier. of London, thus retaining his 
world's championship. 

Mizler towered over thé little 
fighter from Amerlca,- but Sarron, 
with the bell, Immediately commenced 
swinging blows-to the body and 
boxed with great speed, often 
bewildering his opponent. His two- 
handed attack was most effective. 

Sarron olmost knocked Mizler out 
in the second round with o right to 
the jaw. The Londoner recovered,- 
however, ond provided o_ stronit 
finish... He knocked Sarron down in 
the ninth. und tenth rounds for counts 
but the weight of paints 
scored against him in’ the earlier 
stages was too great to overcome.—-

War Planes 
(Special to “Telegraph") 


* New York,-Apr. 15, , 
The 1937 Aircraft Year Book, 
issued-to-day ranks the military 
nir, strength of Great Birtain as 
the first’ in all the world. With 
4,000. fighting planes, of which 
10 per cent. have been built in 
the past. year, and ‘with large 
additional orders being. rushed 
through British factories, the 
Air Flect of the United Kingdom 


[may claim to.rule the skics as 


her Navy rules the seas. 

France is ranked sécond, with 
3,600 machines, and an Increasing 
numbcr of bombers ond ~ pursuit 
craft. Russ{n, with 3,400 craft, in- 
cluding 100 bullt since January, 1936, 
and including 1,200 modern enough 
to be really valuable nogainst oan 
eMeclent enemy, is third on the Ist. 
But it fs clalmed that the Russian 
air force is capable of, expansion 
rapidly -to 10,000 planes. 

Italy possesses 3,200 war plancs, 
immedlately available, compared to 
2,800 in January 1936. Germany, 
with 3,000 ships, compared to ‘na few 
hundred Iust year, has expanded: 
more rapidly than any of the other 
European powers, and her expansion 
gayezon incentive to others. to’ pre- 
‘or“emergencies in the alr, 

The United Stutes has 2,200 air- 
craft in commission with her services, 
—United Press.

Bits To Cut Out 
. _ Brushing Up Bristles | 
‘ Brooms should never be stood on their bristies when they 
are put away in the cupboard for It crushes the bristles 
and spoils them for sweeping. ; ; . 
If-that has happened to your brooms and brushes this 
is how you con restore them, Half fll a kettle with water 
and bring it to the boil. When the steam is pouring steadily 
from the spout, hold the crushed bristles to it. ; 
‘Then rub your hand smartly over the brush, taking care 
not, to lect the steam scald you, and you will find you have 
what seems to be like a new brush. In future keep your 


brushes in a rack, off the ground, or standing on their handle 
ends with the bristles facing upwards, 3 


Washing Chamois Gloves 


TASH doeskin or chamois gloves in warm-soapy water, 

and ict them dry thoroughly. 7 

Pull out each finger,.and press each one with a fairly 
‘cool iron... Then press the palm of the glove, and finish off, 
with the back of the hand. The gloves will look like new.

(HE HONGKONG ‘TELEGRAPH. FRIDAY, APRIL 16, 1937. . 


England Water 
Problem 


Special Committee 
Now, Formed — 


London, Apr. -15, 
The Minister of Health, after 
consultation with other Government 
Depurtments, har appoinied a 
Central Water Advisory Committee, 
under the Chalrmunsnaip of | Ficid 
Marshal Lord Milne, and, comprising 


‘+ persons with a knowledge of the pro- 


blems from, the point of view of 
various interests concerned, to ad- 
vise Government Departments on 
questions relating to the conserva- 
tion and allocation of water resources, 

It is expected the Committee will 
consider, in the firat place, necessary 
menasur's for modernisation of the 
Jaw relating to water supply and for 
the conservation and use of water s0 
that the resources of the: country may 
be used to the best advantnge.— 
British Wireless,

THE FERRY MISHAP 


It requires no great stretch of 
the imagination to-perceive that 
the collision between two “Star” 
Ferry Company vessels in mid- 
harbour on Wednesday evening 
might eusily have been a Major 
catastrophe, resulting in heavy 
loss of life. As it was, thanks 


largely to the presence of mind, 


of the coxswain of the “Night 
Star," - not only were the 
hundreds of passengers: aboard 
the ferry-boat safely landed, but 


Ns. MOUTRIE_& CO.,LTD. 


York Building Chater Road. 


no injury was puifered by any= 
one. The wompany has a record, 
lin the matter of freedom from, 
mishaps, which is a credit to al 
‘concerned in the maintenance of 
n service which carrics some 
v,000 people across the harbour 
every day. There have been 
minor accidents, but this is the’ 
first time in the history of the 
Company in which two of its. 
boats have come into collision. 
Remembering the tremendous 
growth-of the service, the huge 
numbers of passengers carried, 
the frequency with which the 


Alrerries run during the rush 


hours, the crowded state of the 
harbour, with vessels of all types 
constantly on the move, and the 
ocensional spells of bad w lather 
encounteres,, it says much for 
tne coxawaings in charge of the 
boats that such a large measure 


‘lof immunity from accident has 


been enjoyed. -In the instance 


Junder notice, the spontancous 


action’ of the coxawain of the 
“Night Star,” not only in mak- 


ling for the Hongkong wnarf im- 


QUEEN'S. 


mediately after the impact, but 
in readily appreciating the need 
of proceeding to the side of the 
wharf not being used in the 
regular service, is worthy of the 
highest commendation. With a 
sinking vessel on his hands, had 
he made for the other side, not 
only would the service have been 
held up, but “other consequences 
of ‘a serious character: might 
have ‘resulted. All in all, . the 
navigating staff certainly did a 
‘splendid picco of work, tg which 
the fortunate outcome of the 
collision for those on board is 
‘primarily duc. The

Madrid — 


HERI was one word which haunt- 
ed every conversation in Ger- 
mony during © the. inflation period 
“Voluta,” or exchange. In Spain 
to-day the predominant word is 
“gasolina,” for petrol is dangerously 
scarce, ' 

Along the 220 miles of road be- 
tween Valencia and Madrid I saw 
only two petrol stations open, and 
the possibility that the pumps will 
be empty before one reaches them 
gives a.strange excitement to the 
journey across that majestic, wild 
parched country. , 


One result of ‘the petrol shortage 
is ‘thot Madrid is os crowded with 
women and children os it has ever 
been, Thousands have been. distri- 
buted among remote villages, but 
one doubts whether they really equal 
in ‘numbers, those refugees who 
streamed into the capital before the 
Fascist advance from Talavera’ and 
Toledo. i i Ns 

The civillans will not leave partly 
because’ there fs not enough trans- 
port to take them away and, by re- 
maining, they make transport scarecr 
thon ever. - 

he 


- In Vatencla the longest queue I 
enme, across was walling to sea 
Charlie Chaplin In ‘Modern Times,” 
but ‘some queues in Madrid wait oll 
night for a ration of coal. 


This is less terrible in Spain, 
where streets are, above all, places 
for conversatiqn, than it would be in 
England, where they are used: only 
to enable you to get from one house 
to another, ; : ‘ 


‘Yt Is nevertheless .a great hord- 
ship which the people accept with 
resignation because they prefer the 
discomfort and the danger from’ an. 
ocenstonal bomb. to. the terrible un- 
certainties of migration, . 0-60 fo) +s 


There are thousands of families 
whose members are scattered about 
Spain unable to discover each other's 
whereabouts. : 


It is @ tragedy that in the carly 
days somé great international organl- 
sation, reminiscent of the. Hoover xe- 
Hef mission in Belgium’ during the 
war,. could not be established to 
modify the personal miseries which 
so easily lead onc to undervalue this 
eple struggle of a people to win 
freedom. . , . : 

The failure to establish such an 
organisation jis duc partly © to the 
success of General Franco's propu- 
gandn obout “the Reds,” which, has 
dried up the milk of human kind- 
ness in democratic countries, and 
partly’ to incompetence and: distrust” 
of the foreigner—in some cases evcn 
when he Is fighting in the Interna- 
tional Column. : 

I returned to Valerpla carly the 
other. morning and discovered the 
second bed in my room occupied by 
a man I had never seen before., 

He turned out to be a Basque from 
Pampelona sleeping between sheets 
for the firat time in four months. 
He, awoke after cighteen _ hours— 
during which there had becn two 
air-raid alarms with warning sirens 
to which I should have thought only 


‘the dead, could remain indifferent— 


and told me his history. 

His mother was a Socialist, his 
father was a member of Gil Robtes's 
Catholic Party, his brother | was 
fighting for General Franco,, and he 
himself wos 2 Communist who had 
been’ in almost every major battle of 
the Civil War. . 

- ‘The distrust . and crucity ‘which 


-;arlses from such circumstances cons 


nidpiedyat (oeicaad Weteets Duthie 


by 
‘ BARTLETT 


just back from the 
besieged capital 


stitute a tragedy which should win 
the world's sympathy rather than its 
horror, : 

Besides, genuine efforts are being 
made on the Spanish Government 
side to educate ‘and fmprove. The 
summary “bumping-off” of suspect- 
ed or disliked persons has, 03 far as 
I could discover, come to an end, 

THe ignorant guards who used to 
hold you up at the entrance to every 
village.in order to study your pcr- 
mits the wrong way up have dis- .. 
appeared. 


. Their place, when necessary, Is 
taken by smart and eMectent young 
men who have been recalled ‘from 
the front to malntain order. " 
Theso articles refer only to the 
Government side in the Civil War, 
for I have’ not visited ‘the other, and 
prefer: to avoid the ensy method of 
avriting’ down unconfirmed rumour - 
os established fact. : , 
But there is no doubt that General 
Franco jis having: much the same 
diMcultics with hls Fatongists and 
Carllsts os Senor Caballero with his 
Communists and. Syndicalists, ; 
if one did report rumour, ‘onc 
would assert that a prominent Falan- 
gist leader has recently been arrest- 
ed, and thot anti-forcign feeling is 
far stronger in Salamanca than it is 
in’ Valencla, 2 ei 
Whether the Valencia Government 
will win, I do not know. But Ido . 
know, with even deeper “certainty: ° 
than on my last visit. four months 
ogo, . that‘ its defeat’ would be o 
tragedy graver than any other which 
has been suffered by democracy since. - 
the .end of the war which was to: ” ; 
make the world safe: for {ti

“ LAST WEEK 


In the Colony of — Hongkong, ,in- 
cluding the Island, Kowloon and the 
New Territories during the week 
ending: at Baum. on Siuturday, April 
10, there were altogether 43 trafiic 
accidents, as the result of which one 
person was killed and 15 persons 
were injured. Fi 

The person killed, a Chinese male, 
aved 33 years, was knocked down 
and fatally injured by a. private’ 
motor car whilst running across the 
rond. . : 

Of the persons injured, nine were 
pedestrians, who were either walk. 
ing or .unning across the road and 
were struck by vehicles. One tram~ 
car passenger and three bus passen- 
fers were injured while  alighting 
from a moving tramcar and moving 
motor buses respectively. A private 


motor“ear-driver-wos-injured -when- 


his vehicle ran into the bridge rail- 
ing. A public ricksha driver was in- 
jured as the result of a collision be- 
tween two vehicles,

{t for several days.—British Wireless. 


CLOUDY WEATHER 


The anticyclone has moved cast- 
ward to Japan, Ieaving a ridge of 
moderately high pressure over the 
Yangtse. Valley. Pressure is relative- 
ly low over Manuchuria and Indo- 
China. Local forecast: —East winds, 
moderate; cloudy generally.

In Big Mamak

(By “The Pilgrim”) 


Yesterday's hockey match between 
Central British Association and the 
Radio Sports Club, the first of .o 
serles to deelie the Mamak Tournn- 
ment champlonship, wis largely 
spoilt by Me Inexcusable behaviour 
of several C.B.A. players. who diy- 
played annoyance at some of the 
decisions and gave vent to thelr 
feelings with some objectionable re- 
marks, ‘ 

While It is true that some of the 
umpires’ ‘decisions were open to 
doubt, it was a sad reflection on the 
sportsmanship of these players that 
they could not ucgept them In the 
right spirit, This is the sort of thing 
that can ruin tiny, sport. « 

Radio. won -u fast, but not brilliant 
game, by one goal to nil, Awtar 
Singh being the scorer, - 

C.B.A, opened strongly, the Fowler 
brothers and Bickford several times 
threntening the Radio goal. 'T. 
Whitley at centre-half was prominent 
for his accurate passing, and for u 
|time the Radioo were severely on the 
defensive, They appeared to be un- 
settled by playing on a grass pitch, 


made contro! of the ball diMfcult and 
was the cause of a number of mishits. 

When Radio did start to attack the 
forwards quickly proved dangerous 
and C.B.A, had to defend in thelr 
best style/ «|. 

After repeated attacks an opening

We eldest boy's headmaster is retiring after twenty- 


five years of service at the school. 

/ On Saturday morning when [ read the last idntance 
of his printed annowncement:. “It has heen not only my living, 
but my life, and the prospect of retirement fills me with dismay,” 
I was so deeply touched that I immediately wrote him a little note, 
1 knew neither what to say nor how-to say it, but somehow | 
assured him of my sympathy. 


; And on Monday morning his acknowledgment gave me a 
happy beginning for the week, He said: “Your charming letter 
Teminds me, of n bygone age, when sympathy and understand- 
ing were genuine, and people had the will and the time to express 
them.” Sometimes I think that in this metallic, speed-crazy age 
our hearts tend to become streamlined out of our existence,

= ' Bucharest, Apr. 15. 
.The banishment of Prince Nicholas 
by the King threatens a serious clush 
between the Iron Guard and the 
Government. et ‘: 


All the commanders of the .Iron 
Guard to-day attended a special 
council meeting, at which the leader, 


Commander Codreanu, read Prince 
Nicholas’ Ictter to the Crown Councll 
explaining why he preferred banish- 
ment to ‘renunciation of his mor- 
ganatle “marriage with Madame 
Saveunu, . . 


In an’ emotional: setting, Com- 
mander Cordeanu exclaimed: “If the 
Prince is forced to leave the country, 
the Iron’ Guard will show its 
strength.” | 

A deputation from the meeting 
Inter went to see Prince Nicholas, 
who is making preparations to de- 
part, but the Prince refused them an 
audience and counselled the Iron 
Guard not to choese this moment in 
which.to cause trouble.—Reuter,
